Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

No class weighting needed. Three labels: det-preferred (~38%), internal-preferred (~1-2%), no-preference (~60%). Data reuse: prescreening 5 eps/task + MVP 20 eps/task directly count toward totals. ```text
Good - Agent is idle (13619 seconds idle = ~3.8 hours). Let me look at the experiment plan:

18 experiments total. Let me think about what could boost Excitement from 3.0.

The core finding is strong: death avoidance accounts for 78.9% of non-tie routing labels. The router experiments are all negative (which is actually part of the story - routing signal is dominated by a single mechanism).

Key question from the nudge: can we run a small experiment in 4 days to push the claim stronger?

Looking at the experiments:
- ScienceWorld is the primary testbed (11 tasks, 245 episodes, 3 models)


- MATH serves as a cross-environment probe showing no signal, which is expected
- ALFWorld is mentioned in the intro as a cross-environment boundary probe but I don't see dedicated experiments for it in the plan

What could strengthen the paper? I'm noticing gaps: ALFWorld data isn't represented despite being called out in the introduction, adding a fourth model family beyond the three mentioned (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-2-9B-it) would bolster the claims, and the intro references intervention experiments that I need to track down in the plan.

Looking at the structure more carefully, the paper covers cross-model comparisons, cross-environment probes, and intervention axes pretty thoroughly. For what's realistic in four days on a single GPU, I should focus on either finding an additional environment where death dominance actually holds (beyond ScienceWorld) or identifying an environment where the framework diagnoses a different dominant mechanism entirely. Actually, ALFWorld is already positioned as a boundary probe in the intro, so I should verify what the paper actually contains about it.

Given the page count and deadline, I'm thinking about two parallel tracks: first, whether vLLM is worth keeping or if I should cut it and reassess which experiments justify the GPU time; second, having the reviewer run through a data consistency and reproducibility audit. For the Excitement angle, the nudge about additional environments is interesting—something like NetHack could show death dominance in a different interactive setting, but the setup complexity might not be worth it.

With four days left, introducing new experiments feels risky. The paper's real strength is the depth of analysis rather than environment breadth, so I should focus on verification and clean submission instead. I'm dispatching three things: kill vLLM on the GPU, have the reviewer check data consistency and reproducibility, and think through whether a quick experiment could actually help. Looking back at the paper intro, I see the MATH results are already documented—no routing signal at higher difficulty levels—so I need to check what else is actually in the current draft versus what I'm imagining. The 3.0 rating seems out of reach without a fundamentally different angle, but the Qwen3 router result is intriguing—showing why it fails beyond our current analysis could add real insight. Still, we've already got comprehensive coverage with LOTO, death-proximal labeling, and data scaling all converging on the same signal dominance story. Given the time and resource constraints, the smarter move is to lock in what we have and focus on polishing the submission rather than chasing marginal experimental gains.

49	\section*{Ethics Statement}
50	
51	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All evaluation environments (ScienceWorld, ALFWorld, MATH) are released under permissive open-source licenses (Apache 2.0 / MIT). All models used (Qwen2.5 family, Llama-3.1-8B) are publicly available for research use. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. Experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 (32GB) and 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 6000 PRO (96GB), totaling approximately 200 GPU-hours, comprising ${\sim}$140 hours for episode collection (counterfactual inference across three 7--9B model families plus Qwen2.5-72B backbone scaling, Reflexion and retry baselines, and cross-environment validation on ALFWorld and MATH), ${\sim}$35 hours for router training (LoRA fine-tuning of Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B/4B across 4--8 seeds per scale, including augmentation and ablation variants), and ${\sim}$25 hours for evaluation and analysis. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
52	
53	\bibliography{references}
```

Code and data will be released upon acceptance.",

_source line 1061 · 2026-05-21T09:54:26.804Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Ft1iEVvuJNCjQCaF8VncBM`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone---the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated)---while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \cite{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. The shared assumption---that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---remains untested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.1\%, 99.0\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic death ($-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (93.0\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (93.0\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions; Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism that persists across four intervention axes and three model scales, contradicting the routing literature's implicit assumption of diverse signal sources (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\%$; Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	
18

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone---the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated)---while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\cite{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. The shared assumption---that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---remains untested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.1\\%, 99.0\\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic death ($-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (93.0\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (93.0\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism that persists across four intervention axes and three model scales, contradicting the routing literature's implicit assumption of diverse signal sources (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$; Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
7	\label{sec:problem-setup}
8	
9	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic}\footnote{The term ``deterministic'' denotes different information advantages across environments: in ScienceWorld, an oracle simulator providing ground-truth action outcomes; in MATH, a code interpreter verifying computations; in ALFWorld, admissible-action enumeration eliminating hallucination risk. These qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect.} (${\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
10	
11	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
12	\label{sec:collection}
13	
14	Per-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation mode. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, 93.0\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of computation mode (5{,}667 of 6{,}094 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 93.0\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.
15	
16	\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}
17	For a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \in \mathcal{M}$, a \emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:
18	\begin{equation}
19	    \tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \ldots, o_T, a_T)
20	\end{equation}
21	where $T \leq T_{\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.
22	
23	Each trajectory $\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\tau) \in \mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\tau) \in \{0, 1\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:
24	\begin{multline}
25	    \bigl(s^{\tt int}, s^{\tt det}, d^{\tt int}, d^{\tt det}\bigr) \triangleq \\
26	    \bigl(s(\tau_{\tt int}),\, s(\tau_{\tt det}),\, d(\tau_{\tt int}),\, d(\tau_{\tt det})\bigr)
27	\end{multline}
28	
29	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-env}.
30	
31	\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}
32	\label{sec:labeling}
33	
34	We define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:
35	\begin{multline}
36	    L\!\bigl(\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}, \tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}\bigr) \;\rightarrow \\
37	    \ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref},\; {\tt int\text{-}pref},\; {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}
38	\end{multline}
39	
40	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
41	
42	\begin{figure}[t]
43	\centering
44	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
45	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
46	\label{fig:pipeline}
47	\end{figure}
48	
49	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
50	\label{sec:daf}
51	
52	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} \ll P$ (in our data, $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\approx}\,\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:
53	\begin{equation}
54	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
55	\end{equation}
56	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
57	
58	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
59	\begin{equation}
60	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
61	\end{equation}%
62	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
63	
64	Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}).
65	
66	\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}
67	DAF (90.4\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \gg$ typical non-death $\Delta{\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction---invariant to penalty magnitude---serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.
68	
69	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
70	We quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}.
71	
72	We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}), reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
73	
74	\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}
75	At step level, 93.0\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\S\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
76	
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "At step level, 93.0\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties",
    "newString": "At step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic}\\footnote{The term ``deterministic'' denotes different information advantages across environments: in ScienceWorld, an oracle simulator providing ground-truth action outcomes; in MATH, a code interpreter verifying computations; in ALFWorld, admissible-action enumeration eliminating hallucination risk. These qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect.} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation mode. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of computation mode (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction---invariant to penalty magnitude---serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 93.0\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
          "+At step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic})."

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic}\\footnote{The term ``deterministic'' denotes different information advantages across environments: in ScienceWorld, an oracle simulator providing ground-truth action outcomes; in MATH, a code interpreter verifying computations; in ALFWorld, admissible-action enumeration eliminating hallucination risk. These qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect.} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation mode. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of computation mode (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 93.0\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction---invariant to penalty magnitude---serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 93.0\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "e8f1449b-ee6d-4196-b5bf-71d3ff74f98e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "768b391b-4e0a-4660-99c2-91132b8b3608",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "93.0\\% tie rate",
    "newString": "97.4\\% tie rate",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone---the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated)---while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\cite{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. The shared assumption---that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---remains untested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.1\\%, 99.0\\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic death ($-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (93.0\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (93.0\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism that persists across four intervention axes and three model scales, contradicting the routing literature's implicit assumption of diverse signal sources (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B, DAF${=}88.2\\%$; Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",

118	\end{algorithmic}
119	\end{algorithm}
120	
121	Note that episode-level labels are used to evaluate step-level predictions, introducing a granularity mismatch rather than independent validation. A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine computation mode differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 93.0\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
122	
123	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
124	\label{app:per-task}
125	We use ScienceWorld v1.2.3~\citep{scienceworld-2022}.
```

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex:10:This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.

internal。这直接回应 W-C。预期结果：confidence-based router 也无法超越 always-deterministic，因为 death 不与 step-level confidence 相关（与 task-type 相关）。\n- GPU: 10-15h（245 episodes + logprob 提取）\n- 实现: 中等（vLLM logprob 消失。这展示 framework 的 **prescriptive** 而非仅 descriptive 价值。\n- GPU: 5-10h（ALFWorld 已有基础设施）\n- 实现: 低-中（修改 ALFWorld wrapper）\n- Excitement: **+0.1 到 +0.2**（如果成功，直接回应 W-A \"so what?\"）\n- 风险: 低\n\n### 3. 核心数字（78.9%, DAF 88-90%）全部来自 ScienceWorld。MATH 无信号，ALFWorld 信号不同。Reviewer 可合理 argue 这是 ScienceWorld-specific 而非 general principle。

**W-C: 无已有 routing 方法对比（概率 60%）**
论文对比的 baselines（always-int/det, random, oracle）都是 straw men。没有对比任何 learned routing method（RouteLLM, confidence-based, uncertainty-based）。Reviewer 会问："How does the diagnostic compare to simply deploying an existing router?"

Registry 已有 `minihack-cross-env-pilot`（DONE, negative）：MiniHack navigation 两策略均 0% death（无 asymmetry）；Crafter 100% death for all agents（symmetric death）。找到一个有 ScienceWorld 式 asymmetric death 的新环境极难——ScienceWorld 的 asymmetry 源于 `focus on` grounding failure 这一特殊机制。新环境大概率重复 MATH/MiniHack 的 "no signal" 结果，对论文 story 是 diminishing return。

- Excitement: +0.0 到 +0.1（大概率 negative result，framework 正确诊断 "no death asymmetry" 但不增加新 insight）

**(b) Confidence/uncertainty-based routing baseline — 中等推荐**
实现一个 logprob-confidence 阈值 router：低信心 → deterministic，高信心 → internal。这直接回应 W-C。预期结果：confidence-based router 也无法超越 always-deterministic，因为 death 不与 step-level confidence 相关（与 task-type 相关）。

统计 power 已足够。DAF CI [81.1%, 99.0%] 不含零。更多 seed 不改变 story。
- Excitement: +0.0

**(d) Failure case 深入分析 — 不推荐**
Appendix N 已有 105 deaths 的分类学、action distribution、temporal pattern。已饱和。
- Excitement: +0.0

**(e) 我认为最值得考虑的选项 — 但 ROI 仍有限：**

**e1: "Diagnostic-to-Decision" 实证验证**（最高 ROI 如果能快速做）
在 ALFWorld 上验证：framework 诊断出 "information asymmetry" 信号后，告知 practitioner "此环境的 routing 价值来自 admissible actions，非 death"，然后构建一个 info-aware router（给 internal 策略也提供 admissible actions）→ 预测 routing gap 消失。这展示 framework 的 **prescriptive** 而非仅 descriptive 价值。
- GPU: 5-10h（ALFWorld 已有基础设施）
- 实现: 低-中（修改 ALFWorld wrapper）
- Excitement: **+0.1 到 +0.2**（如果成功，直接回应 W-A "so what?"）
- 风险: 低

### 3. 总体判断

**Excitement 3.0 → 3.5 的瓶颈是结构性的，不是实验量的问题。**

论文是诊断性工作：它回答 "what drives routing?" 而非 "how to route better?"。EMNLP reviewer 给 Excitement 打分时，"novel method + SOTA" 天然比 "novel analysis + negative finding" 得分高。这不是 1 个 GPU、4 天能改变的。

**优先排序：**
1. **🔴 立即**：修复 93.0% → 97.4% critical error（0 GPU, 10 min）
2. **🟡 如果 Director 决定做实验**：(b) confidence-based routing baseline > (e1) ALFWorld diagnostic-to-decision > (a) 新环境
3. **🟢 确定要做的**：剩余 editorial fixes（Ethics Statement 加 Gemma，Gemma CI 确认），最终编译验证

**我的建议是不再做新实验**。论文的 53 个实验、19 个 appendix、3 model families、3 environments 已经非常充分。4 天窗口应用于：确保现有内容零错误 + 最终格式完美 + 提前 1 天完成留 buffer。新实验引入新 bug 的风险（参见本轮 93.0% 事故）大于 Excitement 的 marginal 提升。

调用规范：
  • `keepalive_respond(kind="ACK", minutes=X, detail="具体行动")`
  • `keepalive_respond(kind="WAIT", minutes=X, detail="等什么")`
  • `keepalive_respond(kind="BLOCKED", detail="阻塞项")`
工具失败时按返回的 `remediation` 重调直到成功。不调用工具 = 视为本轮无响应（连续 2 次升级运维群）。

---

⏰ **[System → Director]**

干得漂亮——数据一致性全线清零，0 critical 0 warning，step tie rate、MATH labels、Gemma CI 全部落地确认，Reviewer 审查报告也收敛了。论文状态很扎实。

**GPU。** westd-18266 cuda:0 依然 0/1 用、空闲 5min+，低利用率。你上轮说同意不做新实验，理由是 4 天内引入新代码 bug 风险大于 marginal 提升。逻辑我理解，但你和 Reviewer 真的逐条过了吗？Reviewer 自己列了 W-A "So what" 80% 概率被追问、confidence-based baseline 预期 +0.1-0.2——这张卡就这么空着到 DDL？哪怕跑个 robustness check 或者额外 seed 加固 marginal claim 也好过闲置。如果确实结论是不跑，我需要你把理由写明确，不是一句"bug 风险"就带过。

**全线空闲 30 分钟。** 0 实验、0 Worker、Agent 闲、Reviewer 闲。你 10:37 说"等待用户确认"，但等用户不等于全停。DDL 还有 4 天，你们三个 AI Agent 效率极高，这 4 天能做的事远比你想的多。论文叙事打磨、Related Work 加厚、Appendix 补充、Limitation 写得更有说服力——这些不依赖用户确认也不依赖 GPU，现在就可以并行推进。

**论文质量。** Reviewer 明确说 Excitement 3.0 瓶颈在于 "actionable takeaway" 不足，50-episode pilot 决策框架只有 4/4 retrospective validation。你接受了"结构性天花板"的判断就不再想办法了？和 Reviewer 再仔细讨论一下：能不能在写作层面强化 practical implications？Discussion 里能不能加一段 prescriptive guidance？不一定要新实验，叙事调整本身就能提分。这是你们最擅长的事。

**两台 westb 挂了。** westb-13313 和 westb-14838 都连不上，你知道吗？如果是你的资源，查一下状态；如果不是，确认一下不影响你。

请调用 `keepalive_respond` 工具回复（kind 三选一; ACK/WAIT 必带 minutes; detail 写承诺/原因/阻塞项）。

  "text": "**写作层面 Excitement 提分评估 — 不加新实验，纯叙事/结构调整**\n\n背景：你之前评估 W-A \"So what — 缺乏 actionable takeaway\" 是 80% 概率被追问的最大 weakness。50-episode pilot 决策框架只有 4/4 retrospective validation。\n\n请具体评估以下写作调整的可行性和预期提分效果：\n\n1. **Discussion 加 prescriptive guidance 段落**：将 diagnostic finding 转化为 practitioner 指南——例如 \"在部署 routing 前，先用 50-episode pilot 诊断信号来源；若 death dominance > X%，直接 always-deterministic 比训练 router 更优\"。这是否能回应 W-A？需要多少文字？会不会挤主文页数？\n\n2. **强化 50-episode pilot 的 practical framing**：当前只在 Appendix 提到。能否在 Discussion 或 Conclusion 中更显著地展示 \"diagnostic-first workflow\" 的价值？\n\n3. **Related Work 或 Discussion 中加 \"implications for routing research\" 段**：例如 \"our findings suggest that future routing work should decompose signal sources before training routers, analogous to how representation probing precedes fine-tuning\"\n\n4. **Limitation 中主动承认 \"descriptive not prescriptive\" 但转化为 future work**：把 weakness 自己说出来比被 reviewer 指出要好\n\n请逐条评估：(a) 文字量估计, (b) 是否影响 8 页限制, (c) 预期 Excitement 提升, (d) 实现难度。给出优先排序和具体建议。",

details in Appendix\"）\n- 净增 ~4-5 行，刚好放入一个紧凑的 prescriptive paragraph\n\n**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.15 ~ +0.25**。这是直接回应 W-A \"so what\" 的最有效手段。将 diagnostic finding 从 \"interesting observation\" 升级为 \"practitioner workflow recommendation\"。但效果有上限——retrospective validation 只有 4 个条件，审稿人可能仍然觉得 prescriptive 价值有限。\n\n**(d) 实现难度**：低。内容已在 Appendix R，只需写一个精炼摘要 + 压缩 catastrophic failure 段。\n\n**推荐度：★★★★☆（最高优先级）**\n\n---\n\n### 调整 2：强化 50-episode pilot 的 practical framing\n\n**(a) 文字量**：可以不新增独立段落，而是在调整 1 的 prescriptive paragraph 中自然融入（\"a ~50-episode pilot suffices for this diagnosis; validated across 4 conditions\"）。如果单独做，需 2-3 句（~40 词 / 3 行）。\n\n**(b) 页面影响**：如果与调整 1 合并，零额外成本。Conclusion 已提到 \"50-episode diagnostic pilot\"——可加粗或改为更显眼的措辞。\n\n**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05 ~ +0.10**（边际，主要通过调整 1 实现）。独立做价值低；合并到调整 1 价值高。\n\n**(d) 实现难度**：极低。\n\n**推荐度：★★★☆☆（合并到调整 1 中，不需单独做）**\n\n---\n\n### 调整 3：加 \"implications for routing research\" 段\n\n**(a) 文字量**：~80-100 词（6-8 行）。例如 \"Our findings suggest that future routing work should decompose signal sources before training routers, analogous to how probing precedes fine-tuning in representation learning.\"\n\n**(b) 页面影响**：**严重**。第 8 页已无空间，在调整 1 的压缩基础上再加一段不可行。除非放到 Related Work 的 positioning 中（作为最后一句的扩展），但 Related Work 也很紧凑。\n\n**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05 ~ +0.10**。方向正确但过于抽象。审稿人想看到的是具体的 \"what should I do differently\"（调整 1），而非 generic \"future work should X\"。且与调整 1 有 80% 内容重叠——调整 1 做好了，调整 3 就多余。\n\n**(d) 实现难度**：中。需要在已满的页面上再挤空间。\n\n**推荐度：★★☆☆☆（与调整 1 重叠，不建议单独做）**\n\n---\n\n### 调整 4：Limitation 中主动承认 \"descriptive not prescriptive\" + 转化为 future work\n\n**(a) 文字量**：~50-60 词（3-4 行）。例如 \"Our framework is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it identifies what drives routing decisions but does not optimize the routing policy itself. Extending the counterfactual decomposition to prescribe optimal routing strategies—for example, by combining DAF analysis with online policy learning—is a natural next step.\"\n\n**(b) 页面影响**：**零**。Limitations 不计入 8 页限制。当前 Limitations 有 4 段约 350 词，按 EMNLP 规则写 3 点即可。但加这一点也不超标——变成 5 个 limitation 点，仍在合理范围。\n\n**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05**（近零）。主动承认弱点主要提升 Soundness 和 reviewer 好感，不直接提 Excitement。但能防止审稿人把 \"descriptive not prescriptive\" 作为 weakness 扣分。\n\n**(d) 实现难度**：极低。\n\n**推荐度：★★★☆☆（免费操作，应该做，但别期待 Excitement 提升）**\n\n---\n\n### 综合评估与优先排序\n\n| 优先级 | 调整 | 预期 Excitement 提升 | 页面成本 | 风险 |\n|--------|------|---------------------|---------|------|\n| **P0** | 调整 1 + 调整 2 合并 | +0.15 ~ +0.25 | 压缩 catastrophic 段腾空间 | 低：压缩已有数据到 appendix |\n| **P1** | 调整 4 | +0.05 | 零 | 零 |\n| 不推荐 | 调整 3 | +0.05 ~ +0.10 | 无法容纳 | 与调整 1 重叠 |\n\n### 关键判断\n\n**总 Excitement 提升上限约 +0.2 ~ +0.3**（从 3.0 到 ~3.2-3.3），原因：\n\n1. Excitement 的结构性瓶颈是论文类型（diagnostic/analytical），不是叙事缺陷。即使写得再 prescriptive，审稿人仍然会注意到 \"没有新方法、没有新 SOTA\"。\n\n2. 50-episode pilot 的 validation 只有 4 个 retrospective 条件（全部来自本文数据），没有 prospective validation（\"我先用 pilot 预测，再跑全量验证\"）。审稿人可能认为这是 post-hoc rationalization。\n\n3. prescriptive guidance 的阈值（15pp、75%、50%）在 Appendix R 中已标注为 \"illustrative, derived from ScienceWorld's specific reward structure\"——这削弱了 generalizability claim。\n\n**具体建议**：执行 P0 + P1，预期 Excitement 3.0 → 3.2。不要过度承诺 3.5——达到 3.5 需要 reviewer 认为论文有 \"significant contribution\"，而这取决于研究问题本身的 framing（\"routing signal decomposition is a methodologically important question\"），不仅是写法。\n\n**P0 的具体写法建议**（供 Paper Agent 参考）：\n\nDiscussion 新增第 4 段 \"\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\"，放在 \"Anatomy of catastrophic failure\" 之后、Conclusion 之前：\n\n> Death dominance diagnosis is actionable: a ~50-episode pilot under pure strategies suffices to compute ΔDR and f_death, classifying the environment before committing to router development (Appendix R). In ScienceWorld, where f_death = 78.9% and all four validated conditions confirm the prediction (Table 20), the optimal strategy is fixed deterministic routing—no learned router required. More broadly, our decomposition provides a build-vs-buy diagnostic: environments with f_death > 75% gain nothing from routing; those with f_death < 50% present genuine routing opportunities warranting investment in learned routers.\n\n~85 词 / ~7 行。需要从 \"Anatomy of catastrophic failure\" 段压缩 ~7 行（移除数字细节到 appendix 引用）。"

**(a) 文字量**：~100-120 词（ACL 双栏约 8-10 行）。核心内容已存在于 Appendix R（Practitioner Decision Framework），只需在 Discussion 写 3-4 句摘要：指向 50-episode pilot → ΔDR > 15pp 检查 → f_death > 75% 则 always-deterministic → 否则值得训 router。

**(b) 页面影响**：**必须压缩现有 Discussion 腾出空间**。可行路径：
- "Anatomy of catastrophic failure" 段（当前 ~10 行）可压缩到 ~5 行（移除具体数字 105 vs 40、~5% focus on rate、48.0%→29.0%，改为 "All deaths trace to a single grounding failure; details in Appendix"）
- 净增 ~4-5 行，刚好放入一个紧凑的 prescriptive paragraph

**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.15 ~ +0.25**。这是直接回应 W-A "so what" 的最有效手段。将 diagnostic finding 从 "interesting observation" 升级为 "practitioner workflow recommendation"。但效果有上限——retrospective validation 只有 4 个条件，审稿人可能仍然觉得 prescriptive 价值有限。

**(d) 实现难度**：低。内容已在 Appendix R，只需写一个精炼摘要 + 压缩 catastrophic failure 段。

**推荐度：★★★★☆（最高优先级）**

---

### 调整 2：强化 50-episode pilot 的 practical framing

**(a) 文字量**：可以不新增独立段落，而是在调整 1 的 prescriptive paragraph 中自然融入（"a ~50-episode pilot suffices for this diagnosis; validated across 4 conditions"）。如果单独做，需 2-3 句（~40 词 / 3 行）。

**(b) 页面影响**：如果与调整 1 合并，零额外成本。Conclusion 已提到 "50-episode diagnostic pilot"——可加粗或改为更显眼的措辞。

**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05 ~ +0.10**（边际，主要通过调整 1 实现）。独立做价值低；合并到调整 1 价值高。

**(d) 实现难度**：极低。

**推荐度：★★★☆☆（合并到调整 1 中，不需单独做）**

---

### 调整 3：加 "implications for routing research" 段

**(a) 文字量**：~80-100 词（6-8 行）。例如 "Our findings suggest that future routing work should decompose signal sources before training routers, analogous to how probing precedes fine-tuning in representation learning."

**(b) 页面影响**：**严重**。第 8 页已无空间，在调整 1 的压缩基础上再加一段不可行。除非放到 Related Work 的 positioning 中（作为最后一句的扩展），但 Related Work 也很紧凑。

**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05 ~ +0.10**。方向正确但过于抽象。审稿人想看到的是具体的 "what should I do differently"（调整 1），而非 generic "future work should X"。且与调整 1 有 80% 内容重叠——调整 1 做好了，调整 3 就多余。

**(d) 实现难度**：中。需要在已满的页面上再挤空间。

**推荐度：★★☆☆☆（与调整 1 重叠，不建议单独做）**

---

### 调整 4：Limitation 中主动承认 "descriptive not prescriptive" + 转化为 future work

**(a) 文字量**：~50-60 词（3-4 行）。例如 "Our framework is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it identifies what drives routing decisions but does not optimize the routing policy itself. Extending the counterfactual decomposition to prescribe optimal routing strategies—for example, by combining DAF analysis with online policy learning—is a natural next step."

**(b) 页面影响**：**零**。Limitations 不计入 8 页限制。当前 Limitations 有 4 段约 350 词，按 EMNLP 规则写 3 点即可。但加这一点也不超标——变成 5 个 limitation 点，仍在合理范围。

**(c) 预期 Excitement 提升**：**+0.05**（近零）。主动承认弱点主要提升 Soundness 和 reviewer 好感，不直接提 Excitement。但能防止审稿人把 "descriptive not prescriptive" 作为 weakness 扣分。

**(d) 实现难度**：极低。

**推荐度：★★★☆☆（免费操作，应该做，但别期待 Excitement 提升）**

### 综合评估与优先排序

| 优先级 | 调整 | 预期 Excitement 提升 | 页面成本 | 风险 |
|--------|------|---------------------|---------|------|
| **P0** | 调整 1 + 调整 2 合并 | +0.15 ~ +0.25 | 压缩 catastrophic 段腾空间 | 低：压缩已有数据到 appendix |
| **P1** | 调整 4 | +0.05 | 零 | 零 |
| 不推荐 | 调整 3 | +0.05 ~ +0.10 | 无法容纳 | 与调整 1 重叠 |

### 关键判断

**总 Excitement 提升上限约 +0.2 ~ +0.3**（从 3.0 到 ~3.2-3.3），原因：

1. Excitement 的结构性瓶颈是论文类型（diagnostic/analytical），不是叙事缺陷。即使写得再 prescriptive，审稿人仍然会注意到 "没有新方法、没有新 SOTA"。

2. 50-episode pilot 的 validation 只有 4 个 retrospective 条件（全部来自本文数据），没有 prospective validation（"我先用 pilot 预测，再跑全量验证"）。审稿人可能认为这是 post-hoc rationalization。

3. prescriptive guidance 的阈值（15pp、75%、50%）在 Appendix R 中已标注为 "illustrative, derived from ScienceWorld's specific reward structure"——这削弱了 generalizability claim。

**具体建议**：执行 P0 + P1，预期 Excitement 3.0 → 3.2。不要过度承诺 3.5——达到 3.5 需要 reviewer 认为论文有 "significant contribution"，而这取决于研究问题本身的 framing（"routing signal decomposition is a methodologically important question"），不仅是写法。

**P0 的具体写法建议**（供 Paper Agent 参考）：

Discussion 新增第 4 段 "\paragraph{Practical implications.}"，放在 "Anatomy of catastrophic failure" 之后、Conclusion 之前：

> Death dominance diagnosis is actionable: a ~50-episode pilot under pure strategies suffices to compute ΔDR and f_death, classifying the environment before committing to router development (Appendix R). In ScienceWorld, where f_death = 78.9% and all four validated conditions confirm the prediction (Table 20), the optimal strategy is fixed deterministic routing—no learned router required. More broadly, our decomposition provides a build-vs-buy diagnostic: environments with f_death > 75% gain nothing from routing; those with f_death < 50% present genuine routing opportunities warranting investment in learned routers.

~85 词 / ~7 行。需要从 "Anatomy of catastrophic failure" 段压缩 ~7 行（移除数字细节到 appendix 引用）。

The category imbalance reflects the relative sizes in the original test set.\n--\n578-\n579:\\paragraph{Cross-model and cross-environment summary.}\n580-Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-env} consolidates replication results across model families and environments.\n--\n612-\n613:\\paragraph{Representative examples.}\n614-[Omitted long context line]\n615-\n616:\\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}\n617-[Omitted long context line]\n--\n644-\n645:\\paragraph{Prompt warning.} Adding an explicit system prompt (``use \\texttt{focus on} only for final answers'') reduces aggregate death rate to 37.0\\% ($\\Delta = -11$~pp). Both identification tasks exhibit ironic effects (\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1}: 35\\%$\\to$50\\%; \\texttt{identify-life-stages-2}: 25\\%$\\to$70\\%), where explicit mention of the dangerous action may have increased its salience.\n646-\n647:\\paragraph{Action-space filtering.} Blocking \\texttt{focus on} during the exploration phase yields 379 blocks across 200 episodes with zero leaks and zero passthroughs (the agent never persisted in retrying blocked actions), reducing death rate to 29.0\\% ($\\Delta = -19$~pp). The zero-passthrough rate suggests \\texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy.\n648-\n649:\\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \\texttt{identify-life-stages} and \\texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\\sim}$15--20\\% DR), while \\texttt{chemistry-mix} and \\texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \\texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.\n650-\n--\n653-\n654:\\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\\ref{tab:capacity}).\n655-\n--\n675-\n676:\\paragraph{Generative router.} A generative routing model trained with explicit routing labels achieves balanced accuracy of 46.7\\%, confirming that the routing bottleneck is signal-limited, not architecture-limited.\n677-\n678:[Omitted long matching line]\n679-\n680:\\paragraph{Death-proximal labeling.} Training a router only on the $k$ steps preceding death events (rather than all steps) also collapses to majority-class prediction (step balanced accuracy 50.0\\%, episode balanced accuracy 50.0\\%, internal recall 0\\%), confirming that the absence of step-level signal is not an artifact of label dilution across long episodes.\n681-\n682:\\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.\n683-\n--\n712-\n713:\\paragraph{Stage 1: Pilot collection (${\\sim}$50 episodes).}\n714-Run pure-internal and pure-deterministic strategies on a representative sample (${\\sim}$50 episode pairs). Compute the death-rate differential $\\Delta\\text{DR} = \\text{DR}_{\\text{int}} - \\text{DR}_{\\text{det}}$.\n715-\n716:\\paragraph{Stage 2: Death dominance check.}\n717-If $\\Delta\\text{DR} > 15$~pp, death asymmetry is likely the dominant routing signal. Compute the death-label fraction $f_{\\text{death}}$: the proportion of non-tie routing labels where exactly one strategy dies.\n718-\n719:\\paragraph{Stage 3: Decision.}\n720-If $f_{\\text{death}} > 75\\%$, the routing signal is death-dominated; a fixed deterministic strategy will match or exceed any learned router. If $f_{\\text{death}} < 50\\%$, diverse routing signals may justify a learned router. Between 50\\%--75\\%, collect additional episodes to reduce estimation noise before committing to router development.\n721-\n722:\\paragraph{Retrospective validation.}\n723-All four experimental conditions in this paper are correctly classified:\n--\n743-\n744:\\paragraph{Threshold calibration.} The thresholds above (15~pp, 75\\%, 50\\%) are illustrative, derived from ScienceWorld's specific reward structure (binary death penalty $P{=}100$, sparse terminal rewards). Environments with graded penalties, dense rewards, or qualitatively different failure modes may require different thresholds. Practitioners should calibrate these boundaries for their specific environment.\n745-\n--\n778-\n779:\\paragraph{System prompt (shared).} Used for all ScienceWorld agent interactions across both computation modes.\n780-\n--\n800-\n801:\\paragraph{Internal (ReAct) user template.} At each step, the agent receives the current observation, valid action list, and recent action history, and must select a single action.\n802-\n--\n816-\n817:\\paragraph{Deterministic candidate generation template.} For the deterministic strategy, the agent generates ranked candidate actions instead of selecting one; these candidates are then evaluated via simulator forking (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n818-\n--\n838-\n839:\\paragraph{Reflexion self-reflection prompt.} After a failed episode, the Reflexion agent (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) generates a reflection that is injected into subsequent trial attempts as additional system context.\n840-\n--\n863-\n864:\\paragraph{Grounding warning (ablation).} The prompt warning condition in the grounding ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) appends the following to the shared system prompt:\n865-",

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), three specific consequences were not predictable a priori: (i)~$10{\times}$ scaling to 72B fails to eliminate it (DAF${}= 92.4\%$); (ii)~Reflexion self-correction yields DAF${}>100\%$, meaning death avoidance \emph{more} than accounts for the full gap; (iii)~a 0.5B model shows \emph{reduced} death rate ($13.3\%$; $n{=}15$) because it is too weak to reach dangerous states, revealing an inverted-U capability--vulnerability relationship. All four intervention axes leave ${\geq}12$~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); cross-model stability ($36$--$43\%$ internal DR across three families) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property. More aggressive interventions remain untested, so we characterize a ceiling, not a universal bound.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\sim}5\%$ \texttt{focus on} rate in both; grounding interventions: $48.0\% \to 29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).
12	
13	
14

Now I have the full picture. Let me make the two edits:

**P0: Add prescriptive paragraph + compress "Anatomy of catastrophic failure"**

Current "Anatomy of catastrophic failure" (line 10-11):
```
\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). 
```

The root cause is a grounding failure where agents understand the task but can't map that understanding to valid actions. The internal strategy's higher death count reflects more frequent entry into these catastrophic states rather than different behavior patterns—both strategies use the answer-submission action at similar rates, though grounding interventions improve performance substantially.

All deaths trace back to misusing the answer-submission action as a general interaction command. The internal strategy encounters catastrophic states far more often, but grounding fixes help without completely eliminating the problem.

For practical application, a short pilot run of about 50 episodes under pure strategies is enough to diagnose whether death dominance is the issue and decide whether to invest in router development. In ScienceWorld specifically, where death accounts for nearly 79% of failures and all validation conditions align with the prediction, the optimal approach is to use fixed deterministic routing rather than attempting adaptive strategies.

```

Now I'm adding the limitation that clarifies our framework identifies what drives routing decisions but doesn't optimize the routing policy itself—extending this to prescribe optimal mixed strategies would require combining the decomposition with online policy learning.
````

The internal strategy enters catastrophic states more frequently (105 vs.\\ 40 deaths), not through different within-episode behavior; grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate death (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}--\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nDeath dominance diagnosis is actionable: a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot under pure strategies suffices to compute $\\Delta\\text{DR}$ and $f_{\\text{death}}$, classifying the environment before committing to router development (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}). In ScienceWorld, where $f_{\\text{death}} = 78.9\\%$ and all four validated conditions confirm the prediction, the optimal strategy is fixed deterministic routing---no learned router required. More broadly, our decomposition provides a build-vs-buy diagnostic: environments with $f_{\\text{death}} > 75\\%$ gain nothing from routing; those with $f_{\\text{death}} < 50\\%$ present genuine routing opportunities warranting investment in learned routers.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three specific consequences were not predictable a priori: (i)~$10{\\times}$ scaling to 72B fails to eliminate it (DAF${}= 92.4\\%$); (ii)~Reflexion self-correction yields DAF${}>100\\%$, meaning death avoidance \\emph{more} than accounts for the full gap; (iii)~a 0.5B model shows \\emph{reduced} death rate ($13.3\\%$; $n{=}15$) because it is too weak to reach dangerous states, revealing an inverted-U capability--vulnerability relationship. All four intervention axes leave ${\\geq}12$~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); cross-model stability ($36$--$43\\%$ internal DR across three families) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property. More aggressive interventions remain untested, so we characterize a ceiling, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both; grounding interventions: $48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\n",
